6.1.1.3 Generalisation and Overfitting A key challenge in supervised learning is generalization: how well the model performs on new data, not just the training examples. • Overfitting: When a model learns the training data too well — including noise — and fails to generalize. • Underfitting: When the model is too simple to capture patterns in the data. Strategies like cross-validation, regularization, and early stopping help strike a balance between accuracy and generalization. Case Study 1: Problem Context During the COVID-19 pandemic, ensuring proper mask compliance became critical for public health. With Mask - Properly worn face covering 2. Without Mask - No face covering detected 3. Incorrect Mask - Improperly worn (nose exposed, chin only, etc.) Model Selection Algorithm: Support Vector Machine (SVM) • Kernel: RBF (Radial Basis Function) • Why SVM? o Excellent for high-dimensional data o Strong performance on image features o Robust to outliers o Interpretable decision boundaries Training Process Real-time Processing Pipeline Response System Conclusion This case study demonstrates how supervised learning enables practical robotics applications. The face mask detection robot showcases: • Feasibility: Standard ML algorithms solve real problems • Efficiency: SVM provides excellent accuracy/speed trade-off • Scalability: System easily deployed across multiple locations • Impact: Tangible public health benefits The project illustrates that effective robotics solutions don't always require cutting-edge deep learning—sometimes classical supervised learning with good feature engineering is the optimal choice.

6.1.2 Unsupervised Learning in Robotics While supervised learning requires labelled data, robots often encounter situations where labels are unavailable or impractical to obtain. This is where unsupervised learning becomes invaluable, allowing robots to discover patterns and structure in raw sensor data. Example: Robot Room Classification Using K-Means Clustering Consider an autonomous cleaning robot exploring a building for the first time. It needs to understand different room types (kitchen, office, hallway) without being explicitly told. Using unsupervised learning, the robot can automatically categorize spaces based on sensor patterns. import numpy as np from sklearn.cluster import KMeans import matplotlib.pyplot as plt # Simulated robot sensor data from different rooms # Features: [avg_distance_to_walls, obstacle_density, floor_reflectivity, sound_level] sensor_readings = np.array([ # Kitchen readings (closer walls, high obstacles, reflective floor) [2.1, 0.8, 0.9, 0.7], [2.3, 0.7, 0.85, 0.65], [2.0, 0.75, 0.88, 0.8], # Open office (far walls, medium obstacles, carpet) [5.2, 0.4, 0.3, 0.5], [5.5, 0.35, 0.25, 0.45], [5.0, 0.45, 0.35, 0.6], # Hallway (one close wall, low obstacles, hard floor) [3.5, 0.2, 0.7, 0.3], [3.3, 0.15, 0.75, 0.25], [3.7, 0.25, 0.65, 0.35] ]) # Apply K-means clustering kmeans = KMeans(n_clusters=3, random_state=42) room_clusters = kmeans.fit_predict(sensor_readings) # Robot can now classify new rooms new_room = np.array([[2.2, 0.76, 0.87, 0.72]]) # Unknown room room_type = kmeans.predict(new_room) print(f"New room classified as type: <unk>room_type[0]<unk>") OUTPUT: The robot encountered a new room with sensor readings: • Wall distance

6.1.3 Reinforcement Learning in Robotics Reinforcement Learning (RL) offers a powerful framework for training autonomous robotic agents to learn optimal behaviour by interacting with their environments. Unlike supervised learning, which depends on labelled datasets, RL enables agents to learn from trial and error, progressively improving their decision-making through experience and reward-based feedback. This is especially critical in robotics, where agents must operate under uncertainty, adapt to dynamic environments, and balance competing objectives such as task success, energy efficiency, and safety. This section covers two essential algorithms in reinforcement learning: • Q-learning – a tabular, model-free, off-policy learning algorithm • Deep Q-Networks (DQN) – an extension of Q-learning that leverages deep neural networks for scalability in high-dimensional state spaces Both are pivotal for enabling robots to make intelligent, autonomous decisions. 6.1.3.1 Q-Learning: Tabular, Model-Free Reinforcement Learning Q-learning is a foundational algorithm in reinforcement learning, first introduced by Watkins (1989) and rigorously analyzed by Sutton and Barto in Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction (2018). It is a model-free, off-policy algorithm, meaning it does not require a model of the environment and learns the optimal policy independently of the actions taken during exploration. The Q-learning algorithm learns an action-value function Q(s,a), which estimates the expected cumulative reward of taking action a in state s, and thereafter following the optimal policy. Q-Learning Update Rule: At each time step, the agent: • Observes the current state s • Selects an action a • Receives a reward r • Transitions to a new state s′ It then updates its Q-value using: Use in Robotic Control In robotic applications, Q-learning can be applied to: • Grid-based navigation: Robot learns to reach a target while avoiding obstacles • Battery management: Learn when to recharge based on predicted energy costs • Policy optimization: Learn delivery or movement policies that optimize task efficiency Example: Campus Delivery Robot — Battery-Aware Movement Objective: The robot must learn when to recharge and when to continue delivering, based on its current battery level and delivery urgency.
=========================

import numpy as np import random # States: 0 = low, 1 = medium, 2 = high # Actions: 0 = deliver, 1 = recharge num_states = 3 num_actions = 2 Q = np.zeros((num_states, num_actions)) # Hyperparameters alpha = 0.1 # learning rate gamma = 0.9 # discount factor epsilon = 0.2 # exploration rate episodes = 5000 # Reward function def get_reward(state, action): if state == 0 and action == 0: # low + deliver return -10 elif state == 0 and action == 1: # low + recharge return 5 elif state == 1 and action == 0: # medium + deliver return 10 elif state == 1 and action == 1: # medium + recharge return 0 elif state == 2 and action == 0: # high + deliver return 15 elif state == 2 and action == 1: # high + recharge return -5 # Transition logic def next_state(state, action): if action == 1: # recharge return min(state + 1, 2) else: # deliver return max(state - 1, 0) # Training loop for episode in range(episodes): state = random.randint(0, 2) # random battery level # Choose action (epsilon-greedy) if random.uniform(0, 1) <unk> epsilon: action = random.randint(0, 1) else: action = np.argmax(Q[state]) # Environment response reward = get_reward(state, action) new_state = next_state(state, action) # Q update Q[state][action] += alpha * (reward + gamma * np.max(Q[new_state]) - Q[state][action]) # Show final Q-table print("Learned Q-table:") actions = ["deliver", "recharge"] states = ["low", "medium", "high"] for s in range(num

6.1.3.2 xxDeep Q-Networks (DQN): Function Approximation for Large State Spaces Q-learning performs well for small, discrete environments, but becomes intractable when the state space is high-dimensional or continuous, such as in robotic perception (camera images, LIDAR data) or real-time sensor feedback. To overcome this limitation, Deep Q-Networks (DQN) were introduced by Mnih et al. (2015) in their landmark DeepMind paper. DQN replaces the Q-table with a deep neural network that approximates the Q-function: Key Innovations in DQN 1. Experience Replay: Transitions are stored in a replay buffer and sampled randomly for training, breaking the temporal correlation between experiences and improving convergence. 2. Target Network: A second, periodically updated network is used to generate the target Q-values to stabilize training. 3. Loss Function: Use in Robotic Learning DQN is capable of handling: • Sensor-based control (e.g., camera, GPS, sonar) • Autonomous exploration and path planning in unstructured environments • Energy-aware decision-making with continuous state inputs Example: Improvement of previous Q-learning examples to deep Q-learning: We started with a basic delivery robot battery system: • States: 3 discrete levels (Low, Medium, High) • Actions: 2 choices (Deliver, Recharge) • Goal: Maximize delivery rewards while avoiding battery depletion This worked well with Q-Learning because: • Small state space (3 states) • Simple transitions (recharge goes up one level, deliver goes down one level) • Easy to store in a 3<unk>2 Q-table
=========================

Q-learning is suitable for small, manageable environments and can be used to teach robots decision-making strategies in energy, motion, or task execution. DQN extends these principles to more complex, real-world robotic tasks where learning must occur from raw or structured sensor data. Together, Q-learning and DQN form a conceptual and practical foundation for intelligent, autonomous robot behaviour.

6.2 Neural Networks for Robotic Navigation and Decision-Making While reinforcement learning provides the framework for learning from experience, neural networks serve as the powerful function approximators that enable robots to process complex sensory data and make intelligent navigation decisions. This section explores how deep learning architectures transform raw sensor inputs into actionable navigation commands. What are neural networks? A neural network is a machine learning model inspired by the human brain. It consists of layers of interconnected nodes (neurons) that transform input data through weighted connections and non-linear activation functions. Each node computes: Neural networks learn by adjusting the weights to minimize prediction error using an algorithm like backpropagation combined with gradient descent. In robotics, environments are often complex, dynamic, and partially observable. Traditional rule- based systems break down in such conditions. Neural networks allow robots to: • Perceive the environment from raw data (e.g., camera images, LIDAR) • Approximate control policies in high-dimensional spaces • Adapt to new environments via learning Neural networks generalize well from examples and can learn complex patterns that would be difficult to program manually.

6.2.2 Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) for Sequential Decision-Making Navigation often requires understanding temporal context—where the robot has been affects where it should go next. RNNs and LSTMs capture this temporal dependency. Use Cases: • Path memory: Avoiding previously explored areas • Dynamic obstacle tracking: Predicting pedestrian movements • Behavioral patterns: Learning efficient exploration strategies Simulated Scenario Suppose we have a robot that moves in 4 directions: • 0 = forward • 1 = left • 2 = right • 3 = stop We collect sequences of IMU sensor readings (e.g., accelerometer, gyroscope) over 10 time steps to predict what action the robot should take next. import torch import torch.nn as nn import torch.nn.functional as F class MovementRNN(nn.Module): def __init__(self, input_size=6, hidden_size=128, num_layers=2, num_actions=4): super(MovementRNN, self).__init__() self.hidden_size = hidden_size self.num_layers = num_layers # Define the RNN self.rnn = nn.RNN(input_size, hidden_size, num_layers, batch_first=True) # Fully connected output layer self.fc = nn.Linear(hidden_size, num_actions) def forward(self, x): # x: (batch_size, sequence_length, input_size) batch_size = x.size(0) # Initialize hidden state h0 = torch.zeros(self.num_layers, batch_size, self.hidden_size).to(x.device) # Pass through RNN out, _ = self.rnn(x, h0) # Use the final hidden state out = out[:, -1, :] # Get output from last timestep # Map to action space out = self.fc(out) return F.softmax(out, dim=1)

What the Robot Learns This model learns to trust different sensors in different situations: • In darkness <unk> rely more on LIDAR or ultrasonic • In cluttered environments <unk> use IMU to stabilize decisions • On open roads <unk> trust camera vision for direction It builds a robust, generalizable decision policy across varied environments by learning the best combination of sensor inputs. Neural networks are a foundational tool in modern robotics, enabling machines to learn from data, adapt to new environments, and perform complex tasks that are difficult or impossible to program manually. Inspired by the structure of the human brain, neural networks consist of layers of interconnected neurons that transform inputs through learnable weights and non-linear activation functions. In robotics, neural networks are particularly valuable for handling high-dimensional, noisy, and uncertain sensory data. They are used extensively in perception, control, and decision-making tasks, forming the basis for learning-based approaches to autonomy. Key Applications in Robotics 1. Perception and Sensor Processing Neural networks, especially convolutional neural networks (CNNs), are used to extract spatial features from camera images, LIDAR scans, and other high-dimensional sensor inputs. This allows robots to recognize objects, identify obstacles, classify terrain, and navigate their environment. 2. Sequential and Temporal Reasoning Recurrent neural networks (RNNs), including variants like LSTMs and GRUs, are employed when robots must interpret time-series data. For instance, they help in trajectory prediction, interpreting motion from IMU sensors, or planning based on past events. 3. Sensor Fusion Real-world robots integrate data from multiple sources (e.g., camera, LIDAR, ultrasonic, IMU) to improve robustness and reliability. Multi-modal deep learning architectures enable these diverse sensor modalities to be fused intelligently, allowing the robot to maintain awareness even when some sensors are degraded. 4. Control and Decision Making Neural networks can approximate control policies directly, mapping sensory input to motor commands. In reinforcement learning contexts, such as with Deep Q-Networks (DQNs), neural networks serve as function approximators for action-value functions, enabling robots to learn optimal behaviours through interaction with their environment.

6.3 Ethical Implications and Challenges of AI in Autonomous Systems As artificial intelligence (AI) continues to enable higher levels of autonomy in robotic and software systems — from self-driving vehicles to intelligent drones and service robots — the ethical stakes grow correspondingly. Autonomous systems can operate with little to no human intervention, making ethical design, deployment, and oversight critical to ensuring these systems act in ways that are just, transparent, and aligned with societal values. 1. Responsibility and Accountability One of the most pressing questions in autonomous AI systems is: Who is accountable when things go wrong? • If an autonomous car causes an accident, should the blame lie with the manufacturer, the AI developer, the user, or the machine? • Current legal frameworks often lack clarity on how to assign liability when decision- making is delegated to algorithms. Challenge: The diffusion of responsibility in AI systems complicates accountability, making it difficult to trace causality and enforce justice. 2. Transparency and Explainability Autonomous systems often rely on complex, opaque models — especially deep neural networks — which makes it difficult for humans to understand their reasoning. • In high-stakes applications (e.g., healthcare robots, military drones), this lack of transparency can lead to mistrust and resistance. • Black-box models make auditing, verification, and ethical oversight harder to achieve. Challenge: Without explainability, it becomes difficult to ensure that autonomous systems comply with legal and ethical standards. 3. Bias and Discrimination AI systems learn from data — and data can reflect historical, cultural, or institutional biases. • A biased training set can lead an autonomous system to favor or disadvantage certain groups. • In social robots or surveillance drones, this can reinforce inequality or unfair treatment. Challenge: Bias in data and models can lead to discriminatory behavior, particularly against marginalized communities. 4. Safety and Risk Autonomous systems that interact with the physical world must guarantee operational safety, even in uncertain and dynamic environments. • Reinforcement learning agents may find unintended shortcuts to maximize rewards in ways that violate human safety or values. • In emergencies, systems must be able to make trade-offs — but who decides the ethical priority of those decisions? Challenge: Designing AI agents that prioritize safety, robustness, and ethical behavior under uncertainty is non-trivial.
=========================
